1	
2	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. 31	
32	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
33	
34	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude (a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01), so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
35	
36	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.104$ for helped, $\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\max} = \min(|A|,|B|)/\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
37	
38	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: marginally correct predictions are more likely to transition to incorrect, and near-miss errors are more likely to transition to correct, but with configuration-specific thresholds associated with the observed cross-architecture divergence. Cross-architecture threshold differences may partly reflect calibration variation across backbones \citep{guo2017calibration}; however, ECE spread across architectures (17\%) is less than half the disruption threshold spread (47\%; Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}), indicating that calibration differences alone do not account for the observed threshold divergence. Formalizing this as a prediction task (Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}), pure-model confidence achieves AUC 0.70--0.74 within each architecture. Rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (relative spread 1\%), meaning near-miss errors transition to correct at the same confidence level across all three backbones. In contrast, disruption thresholds vary 47\% across architectures (GDN: 0.70, RetNet: 1.00, HGRN2: 1.14 in $|\log p|$), providing a quantitative basis for the observed cross-architecture Jaccard divergence: since each backbone assigns different confidences to the same input, even a shared rescue rule affects different examples per architecture. The rescue pattern generalizes across LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$) and absent on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm} and on binary classification (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}). This structured per-example disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.
39	
40	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As a proof-of-concept application, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\% accuracy versus 50.9\% for majority vote (+5.3\,pp), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are marginal (+1.0\,pp); on HellaSwag and PIQA, routing provides no improvement. Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but fails when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).
41	
42	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.
43

Benchmarks ordered by $\alpha$.}

62	
63	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
64	
65	The per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows monotonic convergence ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association.
66	
67	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
68	
69	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J} \approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 6--7\% of achievable range; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
70	
71	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA: on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
72	
73	Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples; heuristic given $n = 3$, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) support this pattern statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$). This $3\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios, a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects. A Pythia seed-calibration experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provides additional context: within-seed Jaccard (0.27--0.31) exceeds our cross-architecture values (0.09--0.10) by ${\sim}3\times$, though scale and architecture-family differences preclude direct comparison.
74	
75	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.
76	
77	Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and configuration-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity (which architectures affect which examples) reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
78	
79	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
80	
81	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence from $\rho = -1.0$ to $\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$) and reverses MMLU convergence from $\rho = -0.7$ to $\rho = +0.6$, indicating that the MMLU trend was entirely driven by GDN's 16-head configuration. GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The 16-vs-32 head asymmetry could contribute to GDN's faster convergence by limiting attention capacity, though disentangling this from architectural differences would require matched-head experiments not available in the zoo. The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$ while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108. The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
82	
```

We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude (a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01), so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).\n\nEach architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: marginally correct predictions are more likely to transition to incorrect, and near-miss errors are more likely to transition to correct, but with configuration-specific thresholds associated with the observed cross-architecture divergence. Cross-architecture threshold differences may partly reflect calibration variation across backbones \\citep{guo2017calibration}; however, ECE spread across architectures (17\\%) is less than half the disruption threshold spread (47\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}), indicating that calibration differences alone do not account for the observed threshold divergence. Formalizing this as a prediction task (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}), pure-model confidence achieves AUC 0.70--0.74 within each architecture. Rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (relative spread 1\\%), meaning near-miss errors transition to correct at the same confidence level across all three backbones. In contrast, disruption thresholds vary 47\\% across architectures (GDN: 0.70, RetNet: 1.00, HGRN2: 1.14 in $|\\log p|$), providing a quantitative basis for the observed cross-architecture Jaccard divergence: since each backbone assigns different confidences to the same input, even a shared rescue rule affects different examples per architecture. The rescue pattern generalizes across LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$) and absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm} and on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured per-example disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As a proof-of-concept application, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\\% accuracy versus 50.9\\% for majority vote (+5.3\\,pp), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are marginal (+1.0\\,pp); on HellaSwag and PIQA, routing provides no improvement. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. ```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA at 1.3B, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, consistent with configuration-specific pathways rather than uniform rescue (dependent on GDN inclusion; does not replicate on other benchmarks). Aggregate benchmark equivalence thus masks per-example divergence: each backbone exhibits configuration-specific response patterns invisible to standard evaluation. Within each architecture, pure-model confidence partially predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74), though disruption thresholds vary 47\% across architectures (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
4	
5	For practitioners, these results motivate per-example audits within the scope of controlled hybrid linear recurrent architectures studied here: the ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap means architectures interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint examples. A majority vote across three architectures fails to consistently outperform the best individual model (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\,pp; gains on other benchmarks $< 0.2$\,pp) despite 15--45\% disagreement, because roughly half of disagreement examples have only one correct architecture (Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). Oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\,pp of untapped complementarity, but accessing it requires selection methods beyond naive ensembling \citep{wortsman2022model}. Rescue-threshold routing extracts 36\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA (+5.3\,pp), demonstrating that confidence-informed selection can partially access this gap, though gains are task-dependent (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
6	
```

A majority vote across three architectures fails to consistently outperform the best individual model (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp; gains on other benchmarks $< 0.2$\\,pp) despite 15--45\\% disagreement, because roughly half of disagreement examples have only one correct architecture (Appendix~\\ref{app:complementarity}). Oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\\,pp of untapped complementarity, but accessing it requires selection methods beyond naive ensembling \\citep{wortsman2022model}. Rescue-threshold routing extracts 36\\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA (+5.3\\,pp), demonstrating that confidence-informed selection can partially access this gap, though gains are task-dependent (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n",
      "numLines": 6,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 6
    }

```text
30	
31	Beyond LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$), two benchmarks show moderate negative correlations that do not reach significance: ARC-Easy and MMLU (both $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$). Both are classified as ``Mixed'' tasks combining retrieval with reasoning demands. The sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}) shows that MMLU's correlation reverses upon GDN exclusion ($\rho = +0.6$), indicating dependence on a single architecture; ARC-Easy's correlation is more stable. COPA ($n = 100$; supplementary) shows $\rho = -0.67$ with a large $\sigma$ ratio (5.57), consistent with LAMBADA's pattern but unreliable due to small sample size.
32	
33	Several benchmarks show positive or near-zero $\rho$ (BoolQ: $+0.2$; ARC-C: $+0.6$; HellaSwag: $+0.4$; WinoGrande: $+0.1$; SciQ: $+0.1$), indicating that attention does not systematically reduce cross-architecture variance on reasoning or comprehension tasks. The contrast with LAMBADA is consistent with attention's convergence benefit being specific to tasks requiring direct token-level recall \citep{arora2024zoology,arora2024based} rather than multi-step inference.
34	
35	\paragraph{Regression to the mean.} The direction of LAMBADA's aggregate convergence is consistent with regression to the mean: GDN (highest pure accuracy, 0.543) declines to 0.513 under h3-1, while HGRN2 (lowest, 0.484) rises to 0.514, yielding a $5\times$ $\sigma$ compression ($0.024 \to 0.005$). However, three observations argue against a purely statistical artifact. First, the $5\times$ reduction in cross-architecture $\sigma$ far exceeds typical regression amplitudes. Second, the direction is entirely determined by architecture identity, not random fluctuation: HGRN2 accuracy increases monotonically across all attention ratios, while GDN decreases monotonically---a pattern inconsistent with noise-driven mean reversion. Third, per-example analysis (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}) reveals architecture-specific mechanisms: GDN's net $-153$ examples and HGRN2's net $+153$ examples reflect distinct helped/hurt profiles with low cross-architecture Jaccard overlap, indicating that the aggregate convergence arises from structured, architecture-dependent per-example changes rather than symmetric regression.
36	
37	\section{Scale-Dependent Convergence}\label{app:scale_convergence}
38	
39	At 1.3B parameters, LAMBADA convergence is monotonic: $\sigma$ decreases at every attention level from 0\% through 25\% ($\rho = -1.0$). At 340M, convergence is partial: $\sigma$ reaches a maximum reduction of 56\% at intermediate attention ratios but rebounds at h3-1, yielding a net endpoint reduction of 36\%. The trajectory is non-monotonic, and the highest-attention model does not achieve the lowest cross-architecture variance. One possible interpretation is that at smaller scale, the capacity overhead of attention layers creates interference that offsets the retrieval benefit.
40	
41	This contrast is a preliminary observation from two data points (not a scaling law). Despite the aggregate divergence at 340M, mean pairwise Jaccard is similar at both scales ($J \approx 0.09$), extending the decoupling observation (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) to the scale dimension.
42	
43	\section{Sensitivity Analyses}\label{app:sensitivity}
44	
```

50	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.
51	
52	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.
53	
54	\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.
55	
56	\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}.
57	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
6	
7	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\% (pure recurrent), 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, and 25\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\%, h12-1 to 7.7\%, and h24-1 to 4\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.
8	
9	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
10	
11	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
12	
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
15	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.
16	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark; its small sample size renders $\alpha$ estimation unreliable (CI width 0.13--0.18; Appendix~\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).
17	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	All evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.
21	
22	\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}
23	
24	We quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
25	
26	We also report the convergence ratio $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
27	
28	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
29	
30	To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270; comprehension) and WinoGrande (1{,}267; reasoning, two-choice) appear in Appendix~\ref{app:boolq} and \ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\% attention), which maximizes the contrast between recurrent and hybrid modes; \S\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). We classify each example into one of three categories:
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
34	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
35	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
36	\end{itemize}
37	
38	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.
39	
40	To measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true per-example divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures.
41	
42	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
43	
44	All statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.
45	
46	\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\rho| \geq |\rho_{\text{obs}}|$.
47	
48	\paragraph{Power considerations.} With $n = 5$ ordinal levels, only $\rho = \pm 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ (exact $p = .017$); the test has no power to detect strong but imperfect monotonic trends ($|\rho| = 0.9$ yields $p = .083$). This binary resolution means that a single rank swap suffices to shift the verdict from significant to non-significant.
49	
50	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.
51	
52	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.
53	
54	\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.
55	
56	\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}.
57	
```

          "+\\paragraph{Regression to the mean.} The direction of LAMBADA's aggregate convergence is consistent with regression to the mean: GDN (highest pure accuracy, 0.543) declines to 0.513 under h3-1, while HGRN2 (lowest, 0.484) rises to 0.514, yielding a $5\\times$ $\\sigma$ compression ($0.024 \\to 0.005$). However, three observations argue against a purely statistical artifact. First, the $5\\times$ reduction in cross-architecture $\\sigma$ far exceeds typical regression amplitudes. Second, the direction is entirely determined by architecture identity, not random fluctuation: HGRN2 accuracy increases monotonically across all attention ratios, while GDN decreases monotonically---a pattern inconsistent with noise-driven mean reversion.     "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\\% (pure recurrent), 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, and 25\\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\\%, h12-1 to 7.7\\%, and h24-1 to 4\\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark; its small sample size renders $\\alpha$ estimation unreliable (CI width 0.13--0.18; Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nTo move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270; comprehension) and WinoGrande (1{,}267; reasoning, two-choice) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the contrast between recurrent and hybrid modes; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). We classify each example into one of three categories:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true per-example divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\\rho| \\geq |\\rho_{\\text{obs}}|$.\n\n\\paragraph{Power considerations.} With $n = 5$ ordinal levels, only $\\rho = \\pm 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ (exact $p = .017$); the test has no power to detect strong but imperfect monotonic trends ($|\\rho| = 0.9$ yields $p = .083$). This binary resolution means that a single rank swap suffices to shift the verdict from significant to non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.\n\n\\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}.\n",

```text
1	% Appendix sections for the paper.
2	
3	\section{Confirmatory and Exploratory Analyses}\label{app:benchmark_details}
4	
5	The task taxonomy used in \S\ref{ssec:task_taxonomy} was defined prior to running any convergence analyses. Each benchmark was assigned to exactly one of four categories based on the primary cognitive demand of the task:
6	
7	\begin{itemize}
8	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: tasks requiring prediction from long-range context without multi-step reasoning (LAMBADA).
9	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: reading comprehension with explicit passage support (BoolQ).
10	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: tasks requiring commonsense inference or multi-step reasoning (HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, PIQA).
11	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: tasks combining retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands (ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, XStoryCloze).
12	\end{itemize}
13	
14	\paragraph{Confirmatory benchmark set.} Six benchmarks were designated as confirmatory for Bonferroni correction: LAMBADA, HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, ARC-Easy, and MMLU. These were selected as representative of retrieval, reasoning, and mixed categories before any convergence analysis was performed. The taxonomy and benchmark selection were fixed before analysis as an informal pre-analysis plan; we describe this as a confirmatory/exploratory split rather than a formal pre-registration, since no external timestamp exists. The Bonferroni-adjusted significance threshold is $\alpha / 6 = 0.0083$. \textbf{Sensitivity:} correcting across all 10 benchmarks yields adjusted $p = 0.017 \times 10 = 0.170$, further from significance; the main text reports the confirmatory-set correction ($\times 6 = 0.100$).
15	
16	\paragraph{One-sided test.} If one adopts the prior that attention should reduce cross-architecture variance, the one-sided permutation $p$-value for LAMBADA is $1/120 \approx 0.0083$. Under 6-benchmark Bonferroni correction, this yields an adjusted $p = 0.050$, exactly at the conventional threshold. We report two-sided tests throughout the main text as the more conservative choice; the one-sided result is noted here for completeness.
17	
18	\paragraph{Exploratory benchmarks.} Four additional benchmarks (BoolQ, PIQA, SciQ, XStoryCloze) were added as exploratory tests. These are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\alpha / 10 = 0.005$) and reported as exploratory throughout the paper.
19	
20	\paragraph{Supplementary benchmark.} COPA ($n = 100$) is treated as supplementary due to its small sample size. It is excluded from both correction families and reported separately with the $\dagger$ annotation in Table~\ref{tab:convergence}.
21	
22	\section{Convergence Detail}\label{app:convergence_detail}
23	
24	\begin{figure}[t]
25	\centering

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across all 10 benchmarks. Following a pre-registered confirmatory/exploratory split (Appendix~A), four benchmarks serve as confirmatory and six as exploratory, but all share the same 10-benchmark correction to ensure conservative inference.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Six benchmarks were designated confirmatory and four exploratory prior to analysis (Appendix~A); all share the 10-benchmark correction to ensure conservative inference.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\\% (pure recurrent), 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, and 25\\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\\%, h12-1 to 7.7\\%, and h24-1 to 4\\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark; its small sample size renders $\\alpha$ estimation unreliable (CI width 0.13--0.18; Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nTo move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270; comprehension) and WinoGrande (1{,}267; reasoning, two-choice) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the contrast between recurrent and hybrid modes; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). We classify each example into one of three categories:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true per-example divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\\rho| \\geq |\\rho_{\\text{obs}}|$.\n\n\\paragraph{Power considerations.} With $n = 5$ ordinal levels, only $\\rho = \\pm 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ (exact $p = .017$); the test has no power to detect strong but imperfect monotonic trends ($|\\rho| = 0.9$ yields $p = .083$). This binary resolution means that a single rank swap suffices to shift the verdict from significant to non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across all 10 benchmarks. Following a pre-registered confirmatory/exploratory split (Appendix~A), four benchmarks serve as confirmatory and six as exploratory, but all share the same 10-benchmark correction to ensure conservative inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}.\n",

          " \\paragraph{Confirmatory benchmark set.} Six benchmarks were designated as confirmatory for Bonferroni correction: LAMBADA, HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, ARC-Easy, and MMLU. These were selected as representative of retrieval, reasoning, and mixed categories before any convergence analysis was performed. The taxonomy and benchmark selection were fixed before analysis as an informal pre-analysis plan; we describe this as a confirmatory/exploratory split rather than a formal pre-registration, since no external timestamp exists. The main text applies the more conservative all-10-benchmark correction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .017 \\times 10 = .170$). \\textbf{Sensitivity:} correcting across only the six confirmatory benchmarks yields adjusted $p = .017 \\times 6 = .100$, still above conventional significance.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{One-sided test.} If one adopts the prior that attention should reduce cross-architecture variance, the one-sided permutation $p$-value for LAMBADA is $1/120 \\approx 0.0083$.           " ",

"Lessons from the Trenches on Reproducible Evaluation of Language Models." arXiv:2405.14782, 2024. I found information about the PolyPythias project! Here's what the search results show:

The paper is titled "PolyPythias: Stability and Outliers across Fifty Language Model Pre-Training Runs" by Oskar van der Wal, Pietro Lesci, Max Muller-Eberstein, Naomi Saphra, Hailey Schoelkopf, Willem Zuidema, and Stella Biderman, published at ICLR 2025.

The PolyPythias suite is an extension of the Pythia project providing 45 additional training runs across 5 model sizes with 9 different random seeds each. These models enable systematic study of training stability and reproducibility in language models.

The project includes decoupled seed variants, such as 160M models with only data ordering varied (weight init fixed) and models with only weight initialization varied (data order fixed), which allow researchers to separately study the effects of data ordering versus weight initialization.

I found the paper you're looking for. Here's information about it:

The paper "PolyPythias: Stability and Outliers across Fifty Language Model Pre-Training Runs" was authored by van der Wal, Lesci, Müller-Eberstein, Saphra, Schoelkopf, Zuidema, and Biderman, and published in the Proceedings of the Thirteenth International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR 2025).

**Overview:**

The paper addresses the fact that the stability of language model pre-training and its effects on downstream performance are still understudied, noting that the training process can yield significantly different results in response to slight variations in initial conditions, such as the random seed.

**Main Contributions:**

The researchers introduce PolyPythias, an extension of the Pythia model suite trained on the Pile dataset, for which they release 9 new training runs for 5 model sizes, from 14M up to 410M parameters. Using these training runs, they study the effects of different initial conditions on downstream performance, learned linguistic representations, and emergence of training phases.

**Key Findings:**

Their findings examine downstream performance, intermediate representations, and parameter training dynamics across seeds and model scales, suggesting that with some exceptions, language modelling remains largely stable. The new seeds allow them to identify outlier training runs and delineate their characteristics, with findings showing the potential of using these methods to predict training stability.

The paper is available on arXiv (2503.09543) and through the ICLR 2025 proceedings.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nAdding attention to linear recurrent architectures is associated with per-example changes on almost entirely different examples across three controlled hybrid linear recurrent designs: pairwise overlap is at most 10\\%. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Naive majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, suggesting that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden consequences for per-example reliability.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence. All per-example claims are conditioned on this limitation. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$-value is 0.017, contributing to LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ not reaching conventional significance. As a partial calibration, we compare within-seed Jaccard from Pythia 70M PolyPythias \\citep{barak2024polypythias} against our cross-architecture values (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}); within-seed overlap is 2.7--3.4$\\times$ higher, consistent with, but not definitive evidence for, architecture-driven divergence. Future work should conduct multi-seed training on matched architectures to fully isolate architectural from seed-specific effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage and generalization.} We examine three architectures from one model zoo on four main benchmarks (two supplementary). The controlled design isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families. We compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B). Our analysis does not cover Mamba, RWKV, or generation tasks; generalization to these architectures and task types remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics may reveal different multiplicity patterns.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\n\nAll models are from the publicly available model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis code and per-example evaluation outputs will be released upon publication.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

main.tex has no remaining .100 references.

    "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

720	\end{tabular}
721	\end{table}
722	
723	This near-50\% exploitation ratio is structurally invariant across attention ratios: on LAMBADA, the ratio ranges from 51.1\% to 54.4\% across all five configurations; on HellaSwag, from 50.3\% to 51.6\%. Attention changes neither the amount nor the structure of cross-architecture complementarity.
724	
725	\section{Seed-Variation Calibration}\label{app:seed_calibration}
726	
727	To contextualize the $3\times$ within- vs.\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}), we compare against within-seed variation using the Pythia 70M \citep{biderman2023pythia} PolyPythias models \citep{vanderwal2025polypythias}, which provide 9 individually trained seeds of the same architecture.
728	
729	On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), within-seed mean Jaccard is $\bar{J}_h = 0.273$ (helped) and $\bar{J}_u = 0.249$ (hurt), compared to our cross-architecture reference $J \approx 0.10$. On ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), within-seed $\bar{J}_h = 0.312$ and $\bar{J}_u = 0.271$, versus cross-architecture $J \approx 0.092$. Within-seed ambiguity is $\alpha = 0.36$ (LAMBADA) and $\alpha = 0.49$ (ARC-Easy), compared to cross-architecture $\alpha = 0.30$ and $0.21$ respectively.
730	
731	Seeds thus produce comparable or higher amounts of per-example disagreement ($\alpha$) but on more overlapping examples (higher $J$). Cross-architecture variation produces per-example effects on less overlapping examples (lower $J$), consistent with architecture-driven divergence producing qualitatively different per-example patterns than seed noise. The within-seed Jaccard is 2.7--3.4$\times$ higher than cross-architecture Jaccard across both benchmarks.
732	
733	Three caveats qualify this comparison. First, scale mismatch: Pythia 70M is $5\times$ smaller than our smallest models (340M); attempts to use Pythia 410M failed repeatedly. Second, architecture family: Pythia is a standard Transformer, not a hybrid recurrent architecture, so this is a cross-family reference point rather than a matched control. Third, accuracy regime: Pythia 70M accuracy (LAMBADA 0.17--0.23, ARC-Easy ${\sim}$0.38) is far below our models (${\sim}$0.50, ${\sim}$0.72), and Jaccard is affected by base rates; the 2.7--3.4$\times$ ratio may differ at accuracy regimes closer to our main experiments. These limitations restrict the comparison to a reference point for seed-variation magnitude rather than definitive evidence for architecture-driven effects.
734

```text
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our analysis has several limitations.
41	
42	\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\times$ within- vs.\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence. All per-example claims are conditioned on this limitation. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$-value is 0.017, contributing to LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ not reaching conventional significance. As a partial calibration, we compare within-seed Jaccard from Pythia 70M PolyPythias \citep{vanderwal2025polypythias} against our cross-architecture values (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}); within-seed overlap is 2.7--3.4$\times$ higher, consistent with, but not definitive evidence for, architecture-driven divergence. Future work should conduct multi-seed training on matched architectures to fully isolate architectural from seed-specific effects.
43	
44	\paragraph{Coverage and generalization.} We examine three architectures from one model zoo on four main benchmarks (two supplementary). The controlled design isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families. We compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B). Our analysis does not cover Mamba, RWKV, or generation tasks; generalization to these architectures and task types remains open.
45	
46	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics may reveal different multiplicity patterns.
47

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015KQdT2m6vQRd3HkLhCZL9e`</summary>

```text
68	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
69	
70	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J} \approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 6--7\% of achievable range; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
71	
72	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA: on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
73	
74	Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples; heuristic given $n = 3$, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) support this pattern statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$). This $3\times$ gap is consistent with, but not sufficient to establish, architecture-driven divergence; matched multi-seed experiments would be needed to fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects. A Pythia seed-calibration experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provides additional context: within-seed Jaccard (0.27--0.31) exceeds our cross-architecture values (0.09--0.10) by ${\sim}3\times$, though scale and architecture-family differences preclude direct comparison.
75	
76	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.
77	
78	Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and configuration-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity (which architectures affect which examples) reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
79	
```

          " ",
          " On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), within-seed mean Jaccard is $\\bar{J}_h = 0.273$ (helped) and $\\bar{J}_u = 0.393$ (hurt), compared to our cross-architecture reference $J \\approx 0.10$. On ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), within-seed $\\bar{J}_h = 0.312$ and $\\bar{J}_u = 0.354$, versus cross-architecture $J \\approx 0.092$. Within-seed ambiguity is $\\alpha = 0.36$ (LAMBADA) and $\\alpha = 0.49$ (ARC-Easy), compared to cross-architecture $\\alpha = 0.30$ and $0.21$ respectively.",
          " ",
          "-Seeds thus produce comparable or higher amounts of per-example disagreement ($\\alpha$) but on more overlapping examples (higher $J$). The within-seed Jaccard is 2.7--3.4$\\times$ higher than cross-architecture Jaccard across both benchmarks.",
          "+Seeds thus produce comparable or higher amounts of per-example disagreement ($\\alpha$) but on more overlapping examples (higher $J$). Cross-architecture variation produces per-example effects on less overlapping examples (lower $J$), consistent with architecture-driven divergence producing qualitatively different per-example patterns than seed noise. The within-seed Jaccard is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher than cross-architecture Jaccard across both benchmarks.",
          " ",
          " Three caveats qualify this comparison. First, scale mismatch: Pythia 70M is $5\\times$ smaller than our smallest models (340M); attempts to use Pythia 410M failed repeatedly. Second, architecture family: Pythia is a standard Transformer, not a hybrid recurrent architecture, so this is a cross-family reference point rather than a matched control. Third, accuracy regime: Pythia 70M accuracy (LAMBADA 0.17--0.23, ARC-Easy ${\\sim}$0.38) is far below our models (${\\sim}$0.50, ${\\sim}$0.72), and Jaccard is affected by base rates; the 2.7--3.4$\\times$ ratio may differ at accuracy regimes closer to our main experiments. These limitations restrict the comparison to a reference point for seed-variation magnitude rather than definitive evidence for architecture-driven effects."
